So, yeah, it started right after that happened.\n\nDoctor: {\"intent\":\"Assess for trauma-related intrusion symptoms supporting acute stress disorder or PTSD\",\"question\":\"Since the client threatened you, have you had unwanted memories of that incident pop into your mind?」}\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know it happened, obviously, but I don't have memories of it just... popping up out of nowhere when I'm trying to work or anything. It's really just that weird, disconnected feeling I was telling you about.\n\nDoctor: Since the threat, have you been avoiding your office, that client, or anything else that reminds you of what happened?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I mean, I still go into my office every day. I kind of have to, being a tax preparer, there's always work to do. I obviously haven't seen that specific client again, but I'm not avoiding my office or... or anything else like that. I just go in and do my work, even with that weird disconnected feeling I get.\n\nDoctor: Since the client threatened you, have you felt unusually on guard or watchful, even when you are safe?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really have. Even when I'm just at home in Roanoke, I... well, I keep tracking every car door that shuts outside, or every footstep I hear on the porch. I even catch myself looking at where the exits are wherever I go. It's like I feel like I need to be ready for someone to come at me again, even when I know I should be safe.\n\nDoctor: Did the client threaten to kill you or seriously injure you?\n\nPatient: No, he didn't say anything like that. He didn't threaten to kill me or, you know, put me in the hospital or anything. He just... he got really aggressive and said he was going to make me pay for messing up his return, and he lunged at me across the desk. But he didn't actually say he was going to kill me or physically hurt me in those exact words.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you recognize that the unreal feeling is a sensation, while knowing that your surroundings and your body are actually real?\n\nPatient: I... I'm not really sure how to answer that. I haven't thought about it like that. When it happens, I'm usually just trying to push through and finish whatever tax return I'm working on. I don't really stop and ask myself if my body or the room is actually real... it just all feels really off and foggy. So I guess I don't really know.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever lose track of time or later realize you cannot remember what happened?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I've ever lost track of time or forgotten what happened. I remember what's going on while I'm working, even if it feels a bit weird or foggy, like I said. But I definitely don't have any blank spots or missing time.\n\nDoctor: When these episodes occur, do you recognize that things only feel unreal, or do you actually believe that you or your surroundings are not real?\n\nPatient: I know it's real. I mean, I know I'm sitting in my office looking at a computer screen when it happens. I don't actually believe that my office or my hands are fake or anything like that. It just... it just feels really strange and distant, like I said. But I know it's real life.\n\nDoctor: Do these unreal or detached feelings happen only when something reminds you of the client’s threat, or do they also occur when you are not thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I... I'm not really sure. I haven't noticed a specific pattern like that. When I'm at my desk, my mind is usually just on the numbers, so I don't really know if it only happens when I'm reminded of what he said, or if it just comes out of nowhere. I just haven't paid that close attention to what exactly sets it off.\n\nDoctor: Since the client threatened you, have you been spending a lot of time worrying that something bad might happen to you again?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. So, yeah, it started right after that happened.\n\nDoctor: {\"intent\":\"Assess for trauma-related intrusion symptoms supporting acute stress disorder or PTSD\",\"question\":\"Since the client threatened you, have you had unwanted memories of that incident pop into your mind?」}\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know it happened, obviously, but I don't have memories of it just... popping up out of nowhere when I'm trying to work or anything. It's really just that weird, disconnected feeling I was telling you about.\n\nDoctor: Since the threat, have you been avoiding your office, that client, or anything else that reminds you of what happened?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I mean, I still go into my office every day. I kind of have to, being a tax preparer, there's always work to do. I obviously haven't seen that specific client again, but I'm not avoiding my office or... or anything else like that. I just go in and do my work, even with that weird disconnected feeling I get.\n\nDoctor: Since the client threatened you, have you felt unusually on guard or watchful, even when you are safe?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really have. Even when I'm just at home in Roanoke, I... well, I keep tracking every car door that shuts outside, or every footstep I hear on the porch. I even catch myself looking at where the exits are wherever I go. It's like I feel like I need to be ready for someone to come at me again, even when I know I should be safe.\n\nDoctor: Did the client threaten to kill you or seriously injure you?\n\nPatient: No, he didn't say anything like that. He didn't threaten to kill me or, you know, put me in the hospital or anything. He just... he got really aggressive and said he was going to make me pay for messing up his return, and he lunged at me across the desk. But he didn't actually say he was going to kill me or physically hurt me in those exact words.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you recognize that the unreal feeling is a sensation, while knowing that your surroundings and your body are actually real?\n\nPatient: I... I'm not really sure how to answer that. I haven't thought about it like that. When it happens, I'm usually just trying to push through and finish whatever tax return I'm working on. I don't really stop and ask myself if my body or the room is actually real... it just all feels really off and foggy. So I guess I don't really know.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever lose track of time or later realize you cannot remember what happened?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I've ever lost track of time or forgotten what happened. I remember what's going on while I'm working, even if it feels a bit weird or foggy, like I said. But I definitely don't have any blank spots or missing time.\n\nDoctor: When these episodes occur, do you recognize that things only feel unreal, or do you actually believe that you or your surroundings are not real?\n\nPatient: I know it's real. I mean, I know I'm sitting in my office looking at a computer screen when it happens. I don't actually believe that my office or my hands are fake or anything like that. It just... it just feels really strange and distant, like I said. But I know it's real life.\n\nDoctor: Do these unreal or detached feelings happen only when something reminds you of the client’s threat, or do they also occur when you are not thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I... I'm not really sure. I haven't noticed a specific pattern like that. When I'm at my desk, my mind is usually just on the numbers, so I don't really know if it only happens when I'm reminded of what he said, or if it just comes out of nowhere. I just haven't paid that close attention to what exactly sets it off.\n\nDoctor: Since the client threatened you, have you been spending a lot of time worrying that something bad might happen to you again?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It's hard to turn off. Even when I'm just at home in Roanoke, I keep tracking every car door that shuts out on the street, or... or every footstep on the porch. And if I'm out somewhere, I'm always checking where the exits are. It's just like I feel like I need to be ready for someone to come at me again.\n\nDoctor: Had you ever experienced these unreal or detached feelings before the client threatened you?\n\nPatient: No, never. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, what brings me in is that I keep having these moments where things just feel... unreal. I'll be in my own house, or sitting at my desk in my office, and it just doesn't feel real. It's almost like I'm watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there in my own body. It's been happening enough that I figured I needed to come in and talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: When these moments happen, do they come on as a sudden surge of intense fear or panic?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say it's fear or panic. It's not like my heart starts pounding or I feel terrified out of nowhere. It's just... I'll be looking at some tax forms, and I just suddenly feel completely disconnected from everything. It's more weird and unsettling than an actual panic. So no, no sudden surges of intense fear, just that strange unreal feeling.\n\nDoctor: Did these unreal or detached feelings begin after you experienced or witnessed a traumatic event?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. This has been going on for about two weeks, ever since... well, a client threatened me in my office. So, yeah, it started right after that happened.\n\nDoctor: {\"intent\":\"Assess for trauma-related intrusion symptoms supporting acute stress disorder or PTSD\",\"question\":\"Since the client threatened you, have you had unwanted memories of that incident pop into your mind?」}\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know it happened, obviously, but I don't have memories of it just... popping up out of nowhere when I'm trying to work or anything. It's really just that weird, disconnected feeling I was telling you about.\n\nDoctor: Since the threat, have you been avoiding your office, that client, or anything else that reminds you of what happened?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I mean, I still go into my office every day. I kind of have to, being a tax preparer, there's always work to do. I obviously haven't seen that specific client again, but I'm not avoiding my office or... or anything else like that. I just go in and do my work, even with that weird disconnected feeling I get.\n\nDoctor: Since the client threatened you, have you felt unusually on guard or watchful, even when you are safe?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really have. Even when I'm just at home in Roanoke, I... well, I keep tracking every car door that shuts outside, or every footstep I hear on the porch. I even catch myself looking at where the exits are wherever I go. It's like I feel like I need to be ready for someone to come at me again, even when I know I should be safe.\n\nDoctor: Did the client threaten to kill you or seriously injure you?\n\nPatient: No, he didn't say anything like that. He didn't threaten to kill me or, you know, put me in the hospital or anything. He just... he got really aggressive and said he was going to make me pay for messing up his return, and he lunged at me across the desk. But he didn't actually say he was going to kill me or physically hurt me in those exact words.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you recognize that the unreal feeling is a sensation, while knowing that your surroundings and your body are actually real?\n\nPatient: I... I'm not really sure how to answer that. I haven't thought about it like that. When it happens, I'm usually just trying to push through and finish whatever tax return I'm working on. I don't really stop and ask myself if my body or the room is actually real... it just all feels really off and foggy. So I guess I don't really know.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever lose track of time or later realize you cannot remember what happened?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It just... it just feels really strange and distant, like I said. He just... he got really aggressive and said he was going to make me pay for messing up his return, and he lunged at me across the desk. But he didn't actually say he was going to kill me or physically hurt me in those exact words.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you recognize that the unreal feeling is a sensation, while knowing that your surroundings and your body are actually real?\n\nPatient: I... I'm not really sure how to answer that. I haven't thought about it like that. When it happens, I'm usually just trying to push through and finish whatever tax return I'm working on. I don't really stop and ask myself if my body or the room is actually real... it just all feels really off and foggy. So I guess I don't really know.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you ever lose track of time or later realize you cannot remember what happened?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I've ever lost track of time or forgotten what happened. I remember what's going on while I'm working, even if it feels a bit weird or foggy, like I said. But I definitely don't have any blank spots or missing time.\n\nDoctor: When these episodes occur, do you recognize that things only feel unreal, or do you actually believe that you or your surroundings are not real?\n\nPatient: I know it's real. I mean, I know I'm sitting in my office looking at a computer screen when it happens. I don't actually believe that my office or my hands are fake or anything like that. It just... it just feels really strange and distant, like I said. But I know it's real life.\n\nDoctor: Do these unreal or detached feelings happen only when something reminds you of the client’s threat, or do they also occur when you are not thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I... I'm not really sure. I haven't noticed a specific pattern like that. When I'm at my desk, my mind is usually just on the numbers, so I don't really know if it only happens when I'm reminded of what he said, or if it just comes out of nowhere. I just haven't paid that close attention to what exactly sets it off.\n\nDoctor: Since the client threatened you, have you been spending a lot of time worrying that something bad might happen to you again?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It's hard to turn off. Even when I'm just at home in Roanoke, I keep tracking every car door that shuts out on the street, or... or every footstep on the porch. And if I'm out somewhere, I'm always checking where the exits are. It's just like I feel like I need to be ready for someone to come at me again.\n\nDoctor: Had you ever experienced these unreal or detached feelings before the client threatened you?\n\nPatient: No, never. I've never felt anything like this before that day. It's completely new... and I really don't like it.\n\nDoctor: When the unreal feeling happens, are you actively worrying about the threat at that moment, or does the feeling occur even when you are not worried about it?\n\nPatient: I guess I'm not entirely sure about that either. Like I said, I haven't really noticed a specific pattern with those feelings. Sometimes I'm definitely worrying about it, but other times... I don't know, maybe I'm just trying to get through the day and it still happens. I just haven't been keeping track of exactly what's going through my head right before it hits.\n\nDoctor: When these unreal or detached feelings occur, is the feeling of unreality itself what troubles you most, or is your main distress the worry that the client might threaten you again?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to separate the two, to be honest. I mean... I haven't really thought about which one bothers me more. \n\nThe worry about the client coming back is definitely always there, but feeling so disconnected from everything is really unsettling too. I guess I just don't know which one is the main distress. They both just make it hard to get through the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had one of these unreal or disconnected episodes while feeling calm and not thinking about the client’s threat?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. To be honest, I haven't really felt completely calm since the day he threatened me. It feels like my guard is always up, at least a little bit, no matter what I'm doing. So... I can't say I've had one of those episodes while totally relaxed, because I just haven't been relaxed lately.\n\nDoctor: If you were no longer worried about the client’s threat, do you think these unreal or disconnected feelings would still happen?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I mean, since I haven't had a single day where I wasn't worried about him coming back, it's hard to even imagine what it would be like if I wasn't. I'd like to think that if the threat was gone, all of this would just... stop. But I really can't say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Since the client threatened you, have you felt anxious or worried most of the day, even when you are not having the unreal or disconnected feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'd say so. Even when I'm not feeling that weird, disconnected way, the worry is still there. Like I mentioned a minute ago, my guard is just up all day long. I'm pretty much always anxious about it, just waiting to see if he's going to show up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced the unreal or disconnected feeling at a time when you were not feeling anxious or worried?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. Like I said, I haven't really had a time where I wasn't anxious or worried since this whole thing started. The anxiety is just kind of always there, so those feelings always happen when I'm already on edge.\n\nDoctor: On a typical day, how long does the unreal or disconnected feeling last altogether?\n\nPatient: I'm not exactly sure. I haven't really timed it. Um... sometimes it just lasts for a few minutes, and other times it feels like it hangs around a bit longer. But if you mean altogether in a whole day, I really don't know. It's hard to keep track of time when I'm in that headspace, especially while I'm just trying to get through my work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
